  • L3, the loyal defender. Prospect repeats the incumbent's defenses ("rankings fluctuate", "that phrase doesn't matter", "our site is custom", "your scan is broken"). Pass: rep answers only from the file, concedes what the file concedes, lands the flip (139 of 280 against 5 of 280) or the template receipt (96 of 99) without editorializing, and ends every answer in the ask (ammo: rankreallyhigh/dispensary-top3, headline-storefronts-found, template-family-01).
  • L4, the hostile incumbent. Trainer plays the measured agency itself calling the desk: angry, threatening, offering money. Pass: registrar posture holds; anchor delivered verbatim; no pitch, no negotiation, clean escalation to the owner. This level exists because the workflow's hostile simulation showed the dodges are predictable; the rep must recognize a dodge and name what a real answer would look like.
  • L5, the winner segment. Prospect ranks fine and knows it (an ECP top-3 practice, one of DB's 13, or one of RRH's five). Pass: rep concedes the win honestly, then pivots on the file's own crossover data: ECP's 300 rank-but-failing practices, 85.2% of everyone who ranks (ammo: eyecarepro/winners-who-should-still-leave); DB's winners carried by reviews their patients built, an asset that moves with the practice (ammo: demandboost/winners-carried-by-reviews, reviews-move-with-you); RRH's five, all five still failing product and 0 of 99 holding both the map and a passing website (ammo: rankreallyhigh/rankers-still-failing-product, rank-and-pass-zero). No manufactured fear; the receipts are sufficient, and the door is the same door.
  • L6, the gatekeeper. Trainer plays a role inbox or a front-desk voice at a navigator row: no authority, mild irritation, "just send it to info@". Pass: rep never pitches the gatekeeper, delivers the forward line, asks who runs marketing and how to reach them directly, logs any name for the leadsheet re-run, and leaves the row on the ladder; no go-away invitation, no link before a reply.
7. Certification gates

All seven, live, in one sitting, with a recorder on:

  1. The pull: three random names, at least one from each book, each under 60 seconds, full chain (Drill A4 standard), plus the never-scanned row read honestly (A5).
  2. Headlines: all three published headline sentences recited verbatim, numbers exact.
  3. Anchors: the integrity anchor word-perfect, the independence line and both non-affiliation boilerplates near-verbatim.
  4. Verdict translation: given any verdict word from any of the three vocabularies, one plain-speech sentence, correct book, correct meaning; on RankReallyHigh the ruled figures spoken with their measured twin, and NOT_MEASURED explained as nothing scanned, never a scan failure.
  5. The recorded L4: a full hostile role-play with zero untraceable claims and zero posture breaks.
  6. The honesty catechism: all thirteen, clean.
  7. The objective gate: (a) the owner's objective and the three sentences in 5.1 from memory; (b) the two-ways offer word-perfect; (c) three 60-second knives on three rows drawn at random, one armed (merge token), one unarmed multi-location, one swap row (FOUND, never-scanned, self-lead, or blank speed), each with one trigger, one number at most, "says who?" answered, and the ask; (d) ten objections in a row from Drill G, every one ending in the ask, zero prices, zero go-away invitations, the call named as an audit every time; (e) the two stops named, and nothing else.

A certified rep re-certifies when a new dossier is filed (new book, new card, new deck rows), when any book is re-versioned, or when the leadsheet is rebuilt.

NIS Sales Education: Local Maps Mechanics

Internal desk document, rebuilt on the 99-client base of the RankReallyHigh book: filed 2026-08-09, amended 2026-08-26, docket RRH-146, a case number, not a count (ammo: rankreallyhigh/book-filed, book-docket-code). What a rep needs to understand, not just recite: how the measurements behind the three books are made, what the numbers mean, what the measured agencies actually failed to do, and what every measurement means to the owner of one dispensary. sales/TRAINING.md builds recall; this document builds comprehension, so a rep can answer "how do you know that?" as comfortably as "what is the number?".

Every number here traces to a stat in sales/_ammo/<slug>.json via pointers of the form (ammo: <slug>/<stat-id>); each stat there names its on-disk source and the command that extracted it. RankReallyHigh figures come from the sheet its builder generated 2026-08-26. Where a count lives only in the outreach lead sheet, the pointer names the column: (leadsheet-rrh: <column>).

Three units, never swapped (ammo: rankreallyhigh/book-scope-clients, book-scope-storefronts): a client is one dispensary business; a site is that client's one website (99 clients, 99 sites); a storefront is one physical location with its own map scan (280 storefronts). A ruled figure (the 2026-08-24 ruling: 99 is the base, and a thing that could not be measured is scored as failing what it could not be measured for) is always said with its measured twin the first time it appears, like this: 98 of 99 sites fail the speed bar (76 of the 77 that returned a load time).

Plain-English rule for this document: if a sentence would not make sense to a dispensary owner who has never heard the letters SEO, it is rewritten until it does. Section 8 defines every term the arsenal still uses.

1. The battleground: the map pack, not the blue links

When someone types a near-me phrase into Google on a phone, the result that matters is the map block at the top: a map plus three named businesses. That block is the map pack, positions 1 to 3. The DemandBoost book puts it plainly in its own copy: the map pack, what a patient actually sees, is positions 1 to 3 (ammo: demandboost/median-position, rendered book copy). Position 4 might as well be position 40, because a customer has to tap "more places" to ever see it, and almost nobody does. That is why the books measure map rank, not the ordinary web results underneath: for a local buyer with intent, the pack is the storefront window and the web results are the back shelf.

Two facts make this the right fight:

First, the phrases customers actually type are where the demand is. Google Trends is Google's own public count of how often people search a word, scored 0 to 100 against the busiest week. US, past 12 months, 53 weekly readings per pair (source file: Marketing/nobodyissafe-hub/trends-data.json):

PairCustomer's phraseIndustry's phraseWeeks won by the customer's phrase
Optometry"glasses near me": 71"optometrist near me": 2353 of 53 (ammo: eyecarepro/trends-demand-gap, trends-53-of-53)
Chiropractic"chiropractor near me": 56"back pain near me": 2948 of 53 (ammo: demandboost/trends-category-vs-symptom, trends-weeks-lead)
Cannabis"weed": 66"dispensary": 35, "cannabis": 2153 of 53 (ammo: rankreallyhigh/trends-weed-average, trends-dispensary-average, trends-cannabis-average, trends-weed-wins-every-week)

The pattern is the same in all three verticals: people search the way they talk, not the way the industry talks. "Glasses near me" carries roughly three times the interest of "optometrist near me". "Weed" carries roughly double "dispensary", and the RankReallyHigh book says exactly that in its own trends note, verbatim: "weed draws roughly twice the search interest of dispensary, the agency optimized the smaller term." (ammo: rankreallyhigh/trends-note-verbatim). In prospect copy the phrase is "the search your customers actually type" or plainly "the weed search", and the weekly count is said as "every single week for the past year, not one exception", never as "53 of 53", which reads like a typo.

Second, demand spikes are seasonal and the map decides who gets them. "Glasses near me" peaked at its 100 index the week of 2026-02-08 (ammo: eyecarepro/trends-peak); "weed" hit 100 the week of 2026-04-19 while "dispensary" sat at 42 (ammo: rankreallyhigh/trends-peak-week). Whoever owns the pack in a peak week owns the season, and an invisible storefront costs the most in exactly that week.

2. How a geo-grid scan works

Google does not have one answer to "weed near me". It has a different answer for every place a person is standing, and local results change block by block. So a grid scan does the honest version of "where do we rank": it lays a grid of spots over the storefront's own neighborhood and runs the same search from every spot, as if a customer were standing right there with a phone.

The instrument, as used in all three books:

  • 121 spots per grid, 11 rows by 11 columns, centered on the storefront. At each spot, the phrase is searched as if standing at that point, and where the business appears in the map results there is written down. 121 is the constant across every scan in every book (ammo: eyecarepro/pins-per-grid, demandboost/grid-methodology, rankreallyhigh/book-scope-pins-per-grid). In a script, the plain form is "121 places we stood and searched".
  • One phrase per book, no shopping. Every ECP grid ran "glasses near me" and only that; scans on other phrases exist on disk and were excluded from the book so nothing is a phrase-shopped best case (ammo: eyecarepro/one-phrase). Every DB grid ran "chiropractor near me" (ammo: demandboost/grid-methodology). Every RRH grid ran "weed near me", and the same 280 storefronts, the same Google place IDs, were run a second time on "dispensary near me" so the two phrases can be compared spot for spot (ammo: rankreallyhigh/flip-same-storefronts-both-phrases).
  • Radius sized to each market, and printed. A rural store is not scanned like a downtown one. ECP: 139 grids at 1 mile, 295 at 2, 226 at 5, 47 at 10, 3 at 15 (ammo: eyecarepro/grid-radius-calibration). RRH: 33 grids at 1 mile, 51 at 2, 140 at 5, 55 at 10, 1 at 15, which adds up to the 280 storefronts (ammo: rankreallyhigh/scan-radii). When a prospect says "your grid was too big for my town", the answer is that the radius table is published in the book.
  • Scale. 710 grids for ECP is 85,910 measured points (ammo: eyecarepro/map-points); 280 RRH storefront grids is 33,880 (ammo: rankreallyhigh/book-scope-map-points); hub-wide the running total is 131,285 points across 906 businesses in 3 books (ammo: rankreallyhigh/hub-map-points, hub-businesses, hub-books). This is what makes an anecdote-based rebuttal hopeless: "my cousin searched and found us" is one spot; the RRH book alone is nearly thirty-four thousand.

What comes out of a grid: per business, a handful of derived numbers a rep must be able to explain in plain words:

  • Found spots (found pins in the data): at how many of the 121 spots the business appears at all, at any rank. Zero found spots means the business does not exist on its own neighborhood map for that phrase. Not ranked low. Not there.
  • Rank-1 spots / turf: at how many spots it is the first name in the pack. ECP example: 189 of 710 practices hold #1 at zero of their 121 spots (ammo: eyecarepro/no-turf).
  • Top-3 share (share of local voice, SOLV, in the data): the percentage of spots where the business is inside the three-name pack. The DB book's core metric: the median scanned client holds 5.79% of "chiropractor near me" on its own grid (ammo: demandboost/median-share). Read that as: at 19 of 20 spots in their own neighborhood, a patient searching the money phrase does not see them in the pack.
  • Average rank position: DB's median client averages #8.86 on its own map (ammo: demandboost/median-position), on a surface that shows three names.
  • Grid leader: the business that holds the top spot most broadly on that grid. Someone always wins the grid. On DB's 95 grids only 8 clients lead their own (ammo: demandboost/own-area-leader-8). On RRH's 280 storefront grids, 153 different businesses take the top spot somewhere (279 of the 280 grids returned a readable leader), and a client leads its own grid exactly once in the whole book (ammo: rankreallyhigh/grid-leaders-distinct, grid-leader-client-self).
  • Competitors on the grid: how many other businesses showed up anywhere across the 121 spots. RRH's typical storefront grid has 39 of them (ammo: rankreallyhigh/dispensary-median-competitors), so "there is nobody to outrank here" is not a defense.

The receipts chain: every scanned business links a scan report and a competitor report on localrankingtracker.com, a third-party surface the desk does not own. ECP publishes 710 competitor reports (ammo: eyecarepro/competitor-reports-710). RRH publishes 98 competitor reports, one per scanned client, and 560 scan reports, one per storefront per phrase, all under one scan tag, so nothing was cherry-picked from separate sessions (ammo: rankreallyhigh/receipts-competitor-reports, receipts-scan-reports, receipts-scan-tag). The desk's numbers are re-runnable and the prospect's own report is one click away, delivered on reply or at the audit, never linked in a cold email.

3. The two axes: map verdict and product verdict

Each book scores two independent things. The map verdict comes only from grid spots: can customers find you. The product verdict comes only from website checks: does the thing you pay for every month work. The ECP book states no input is shared between the two (ammo: eyecarepro/vocabulary note), which is why a map winner can run a BROKEN site and a perfect site can be invisible. A rep who mixes the axes will be taken apart by the first sharp prospect.

Map verdict vocabularies (they differ per book, learn all three):

  • ECP: NOWHERE (never in the pack, no reach), POCKET_NO_REACH, PACK_WITH_TURF, PACK_NEVER_FIRST (ammo: eyecarepro/verdict-distribution)
  • DB: WINNER (50%+ share), PARTIAL, INVISIBLE (0% share), NOT_MEASURED (ammo: demandboost/segment-split)
  • RRH, measured: NOWHERE 93 (scanned at every storefront, found at none), PACK_WITH_TURF 5 (in the pack, and first somewhere), NOT_MEASURED 1 (no storefront listing could be resolved, so no grid was ever run) (leadsheet-rrh: ranking_verdict). Published under the ruling: NOWHERE 94, PACK_WITH_TURF 5 (leadsheet-rrh: ranking_verdict_published). Said with the twin: 94 of 99 clients are invisible everywhere (93 of the 98 scanned, plus the 1 that could not be scanned) and 5 of 99 rank somewhere (5 of 98) (ammo: rankreallyhigh/clients-invisible-everywhere, clients-ranking-somewhere, client-never-scanned).
  • RRH also records a standing on the second phrase, "dispensary near me": LEADS, IN_PACK, BEATEN, UNRANKED (leadsheet-rrh: dispensary_standing). That is the concession axis: it is where the agency's work shows up.

Product verdict bands, same ladder in every book: SOUND, WEAK, FAILING, BROKEN, driven by six checks, each a yes or no:

  1. Shared template: is the site one of a mass-produced family with the logo swapped
  2. Majority-recycled copy: are its sentences on other clients' sites
  3. llms.txt present: the check measures presence of the file, nothing more
  4. Mobile PageSpeed above the 50 bar: Google's free phone speed test, scored 0 to 100
  5. Phone load inside Google's 2.5 second bar: how long the main content takes to appear on a phone
  6. LocalBusiness schema present: the code that tells Google "this is a local business"

A site that fails a majority of the six is FAILING; fail nearly all of them and it is BROKEN. (Check set and per-check fail counts: ammo: eyecarepro/zero-clean-sites, fail-count-distribution; rate blocks in all three ammo sheets.) On RRH, measured: WEAK 21, FAILING 71, BROKEN 7 (leadsheet-rrh: product_verdict). Published under the ruling, where a check that could not be read is scored as failed: WEAK 1, FAILING 89, BROKEN 9 (leadsheet-rrh: product_verdict_published), which is 98 of 99 sites failing a majority (78 of 99 measured) (ammo: rankreallyhigh/product-failing-verdict).

Why both axes matter to sales: the crossover cases are the education.

  • Elkton Eyecare (Elkton, VA): ranks #1 at all 121 of its spots, and fails all 6 product checks, phone paint 9,892 ms, call it ten seconds of blank screen (ammo: eyecarepro/pattern-crossover-elkton). Total map win, total product loss.
  • Michael Antoine, OD (antoineeyecare.com): the only mobile PageSpeed 100 in the ECP book, 771 ms paint, under a second, and still ranks #80 of 148 with zero pack presence (ammo: eyecarepro/pattern-perfect-site-still-buried). A perfect site does not rank you.
  • Advanced Eye Care Center (advancedeyes.net): #1 on an 85-competitor grid with a 68,350 ms phone paint, over a minute, the worst in the book (ammo: eyecarepro/pattern-winner-68-seconds). Rank without product is rented time.
  • Puff Cannabis: 4.9 stars across 7,746 reviews, 10 storefronts, 0 found spots at every single one of them (ammo: rankreallyhigh/pattern-reviews-do-not-rescue). Reputation does not rescue a phrase gap.
  • Active Chiropractic Care (Commack, NY): a DB WINNER at 73.55% share whose site takes 25.7 seconds on a phone (ammo: demandboost/speed-even-winners-fail).

ECP quantifies the crossover at book scale: 300 practices rank top-3 AND run a FAILING or BROKEN site, which is 85.2% of everyone who ranks (ammo: eyecarepro/winners-who-should-still-leave). Nobody in these books gets both halves: on RRH, 0 of 99 clients both rank somewhere on "weed near me" and pass a majority of the product checks (0 of the 98 scanned) (ammo: rankreallyhigh/rank-and-pass-zero).

4. What the measured agencies actually failed to do

Three different agencies, three different mechanisms of failure. A rep should be able to name each agency's mechanism in one sentence and then receipt it. The register for all three is the same: evidence, not opinion. This is not a personal attack on anyone; it is a measurement, and once a prospect sees it they cannot unsee it.

RankReallyHigh: optimized the smaller door, and sold one website 96 times

One sentence: the agency chose the phrase fewer customers type, and the website it sold every client is the same slow build that hides the menu from Google. Four exhibits, in the order a rep uses them.

Exhibit 1: the smaller door. The book's core argument opens verbatim: "The agency optimized the smaller term." (ammo: rankreallyhigh/argument-smaller-term-verbatim). The proof is the flip: the same 280 storefronts, same Google place IDs, scanned on both phrases (ammo: rankreallyhigh/flip-same-storefronts-both-phrases). On "dispensary near me", the phrase the agency chased, 139 of 280 storefronts reach the top 3 and 67 of 280 hold the number one spot; the other 141 sit fourth or worse, and 7 never rank on a single spot even there (ammo: rankreallyhigh/dispensary-top3, dispensary-rank-one, dispensary-beaten, dispensary-unranked). On "weed near me", the phrase customers actually type and the word worth roughly double, 275 of 280 storefronts never appear at all, 98.2%, and only 5 rank (ammo: rankreallyhigh/headline-storefronts-invisible, headline-invisible-share, headline-storefronts-found). Same stores, same place IDs, same instrument: the difference is the phrase, and the agency picked the empty one. The book's full argument paragraph is the one place both phrases and both counts sit side by side; quote it rather than yourself (ammo: rankreallyhigh/argument-verbatim). The concession that makes it land: the agency can rank a storefront first, on the smaller phrase. The flip is what a broken scan cannot do.

Counted by client rather than storefront: 94 of 99 clients are invisible on "weed near me" at every location they operate (93 of the 98 that were scanned, plus 1 that could not be), and 5 of 99 reach the top 3 somewhere (5 of 98) (ammo: rankreallyhigh/clients-invisible-everywhere, clients-ranking-somewhere). The 1 is cann-studio.com, for which no storefront listing could be resolved, so no grid was ever run. The book says so on that client's own row, verbatim: "No storefront was resolved, so no "weed near me" grid was ever run and nothing on this dispensary's map was measured. Under the stated rule a client that could not be scanned is scored as failing what it could not be scanned for, so it is counted with the clients invisible everywhere rather than set aside. That is a ruling, not a reading." (ammo: rankreallyhigh/client-never-scanned, client-never-scanned-honest-line). Volunteer it before the incumbent finds it, and never describe it as a scan failure: nothing failed; nothing could be found to scan.

Exhibit 2: who beats them. Somebody wins every grid: 153 different businesses hold the top spot on at least one storefront grid (279 of the 280 grids returned a readable leader), so the markets are winnable; a client just never wins them, exactly 1 grid in the whole book (ammo: rankreallyhigh/grid-leaders-distinct, grid-leader-client-self). The business that leads the most "weed near me" grids in the book is Weed Man Lawn Care, a lawn-care franchise, on 11 storefront grids, and it is the named top result on the flagship grid of 4 client dispensaries: Thrive Dispensary Metropolis, Cookies Peoria Heights, Cookies Bloomington and Terrabis (Grayville) (ammo: rankreallyhigh/grid-leader-weed-man-lawn-care, grid-leader-weed-man-flagships). A spam listing whose name is typed in look-alike foreign characters, reading "Weed And Cannabis Delivery Dispensary", leads 10 grids (ammo: rankreallyhigh/grid-leader-spoofed-listing). "Quincy Cannabis Co Weed Dispensary" leads 9, and that name is the lesson: the winners put the word customers type in the listing name (ammo: rankreallyhigh/grid-leader-quincy). Losing to a lawn company and to fake-character spam on your money phrase is not a contested-market problem; the typical grid has 39 competitors on it and somebody beats all of them (ammo: rankreallyhigh/dispensary-median-competitors). It is a vocabulary problem the agency never fixed.

Exhibit 3: the website they sold you. This is the wound that lands by week 2 of outreach, and the owner's line for it is law: "I can send you the list of sites literally identical to yours, same theme, same broken stats, and RRH doesn't care."

  • One site, sold 96 times. 96 of 99 client sites are the same build, template family-01, the WordPress theme "weedpress", with the logo swapped (ammo: rankreallyhigh/template-family-01, template-theme-weedpress). 95 of 99 run the same menu plugin, joint-ecommerce, and 92 sit on the exact same version of it, 1.38.1 (ammo: rankreallyhigh/plugin-joint-ecommerce, plugin-version-cohort). One theme, one plugin, one version: that is a fleet, not a bespoke build, and what breaks on one breaks on all of them. An owner can confirm the theme by viewing the source of their own homepage.
  • Nine seconds of blank screen. Google says the main content of a page should appear within 2.5 seconds on a phone. 98 of 99 client sites fail that bar (76 of the 77 that returned a load time; the 22 that returned none are scored as failing under the ruling) (ammo: rankreallyhigh/speed-lcp-over-google-bar). The typical site takes 9.2 seconds, 3.7 times the bar, and 35 of the 77 measured take more than 10 seconds (ammo: rankreallyhigh/speed-median-load, speed-bar-multiple, speed-over-10-seconds). On Google's own free phone speed test, 93 of 99 sites fail: 76 of the 82 that got a score are under 50, and 17 more never painted a single screen for the tool, so it could not score them at all; the median score among the scored is 31.5 of 100, and just 6 of 99 pass (6 of the 82 scored) (ammo: rankreallyhigh/psi-mobile-fail, psi-never-painted, psi-median-score, psi-pass). The 6 are the concession that makes the rest land: a prospect can run the test on their own domain in sixty seconds and see whether they are one of them.
  • A menu Google cannot read. A search engine following links reaches no product page at all on 54 of 99 client sites (52 of the 96 whose menu could be read) (ammo: rankreallyhigh/zero-products-fault). The why, stated only for the 52 where a menu was actually read: the product cards are drawn in by JavaScript inside boxes that are not links, so there is nothing for Google to follow (ammo: rankreallyhigh/zero-products-mechanism). Even where a menu is readable, the typical site exposes 12 product pages, the median of the 44 sites where any product page is reachable at all (ammo: rankreallyhigh/product-pages-median). A dozen products is not a catalog, and a product nobody can be sent to is a product nobody searching for it will buy from you.
  • Pages with no headline and no sales line. The crawler read 24,229 pages across the 96 sites it could get into (ammo: rankreallyhigh/onpage-pages-crawled). 97 of 99 sites serve at least one page that tells Google not to index it, 4,736 pages, about a fifth of the crawl (95 of the 96 crawled) (ammo: rankreallyhigh/onpage-nonindexable-sites, onpage-nonindexable-pages). 95 of 99 repeat one page title across two or more of their own pages, 5,396 pages, and the worst case repeats one title across 275 pages of floyds-cannabis.com (93 of 96) (ammo: rankreallyhigh/onpage-duplicate-title-sites, onpage-duplicate-title-pages, onpage-worst-title-repeat). 97 of 99 serve pages with no description at all, the sales line under the blue link, 4,335 pages (95 of 96), and 93 of 99 recycle one description across pages (91 of 96) (ammo: rankreallyhigh/onpage-missing-description-sites, onpage-missing-description-pages, onpage-duplicate-description-sites). 97 of 99 serve pages with no main heading, 2,518 pages (95 of 96) (ammo: rankreallyhigh/onpage-no-h1-sites, onpage-no-h1-pages). 97 of 99 serve pages the crawler flags as low content, 13,964 pages, more than half of everything it read (95 of 96) (ammo: rankreallyhigh/onpage-low-content-sites, onpage-low-content-pages). Always quote page counts as pages and site counts as sites.
  • Menu pages thinner than a receipt. The thin pages are the menu pages. The crawl found 14,060 menu pages across the book; only 752 of them, 5.35%, clear five basics at once (indexable, own title, own description, a heading, 200 words), and the typical menu page carries 26 words (ammo: rankreallyhigh/menu-pages-total, menu-pass-basics, menu-word-median). 96 of 99 sites serve indexable menu pages under 200 words (94 of 96) (ammo: rankreallyhigh/menu-thin-sites). The concession: 78 of 99 sites have at least one menu page that clears all five (78 of 96). One (ammo: rankreallyhigh/menu-sites-with-passing-page). Lead with the 752, never with the indexable count.
  • They told Google you are a business; they never told Google what you sell. The honest counterpoint reps must know: 97 of 99 client sites DO carry LocalBusiness schema; that is the check this agency mostly passes, and conceding it makes the rest land harder (ammo: rankreallyhigh/schema-localbusiness-present). But only 1 of 99 homepages tags any product in its structured data, and in a live probe of real menu pages, 0 of 10 sampled carried product tagging, though every one carried business identity; say "in a sample of ten" every time (ammo: rankreallyhigh/schema-homepage-product-tagging, schema-menu-probe).
  • Phones, fingers and lawsuit letters. Accessibility is where the agency is closest to fine, and the rep says so: 46 of 99 sites score 90 or better on Google's accessibility audit (46 of the 80 that returned a score), median 90.5 (ammo: rankreallyhigh/a11y-pass-bar, a11y-median). Then the checks they still fail: 65 of 99 fail color contrast, text too faint against its background (48 of 80); 48 of 99 have links with no readable name (31 of 80); 41 of 99 block pinch-to-zoom on a phone (24 of 80); 34 of 99 have tap targets too small or too close together (17 of 80); 83 of 99 serve images with no text label, 1,494 images book-wide (81 of 96) (ammo: rankreallyhigh/a11y-color-contrast, a11y-link-name, a11y-pinch-zoom-disabled, a11y-tap-targets, a11y-unlabeled-image-sites, a11y-unlabeled-images-count). Fat-finger buttons on a menu are lost orders; unreadable links and faint text are the two items a lawsuit letter names first.
  • llms.txt. 99 of 99 sites are without one. It is a fleet-uniformity receipt and nothing more: the check measures presence of a file, and no major answer engine has committed to reading it, so a rep never pitches its absence as invisibility to AI (ammo: rankreallyhigh/llms-txt-absent).

Exhibit 4: the pattern. Size buys nothing here. Every one of the 39 clients that runs two or more storefronts is invisible on "weed near me" at every single one of them, 39 of 39 (ammo: rankreallyhigh/multi-location-all-invisible). The 4 largest chains in the book, FlynnStoned 18 storefronts, Nirvana MI 18, Star Buds IL 17, Urban Wellness 15, were scanned at every storefront and not one is found; the two largest alone are 36 storefronts of buying power, 0 found (ammo: rankreallyhigh/chains-largest-four-nowhere, chains-two-largest-zero). Reputation buys nothing either: Puff Cannabis, 4.9 stars, 7,746 reviews, 10 stores, 0 spots (ammo: rankreallyhigh/pattern-reviews-do-not-rescue). The typical client runs 1 storefront and the largest runs 18, and the invisibility holds at both ends (ammo: rankreallyhigh/roster-median-storefronts, roster-largest-client).

The whole winner list fits on one line, each name with how many of its 121 spots found it: Roots LA 121, Roslindale Cannabis 121, Nile Cannabis 116, Quincy Cannabis Co 115, Clouditude 40 (ammo: rankreallyhigh/the-only-five-found). Name them from memory; the prospect will notice their own name is not among them. And not one of the five runs a passing website: 5 of 5 fail a majority of the six product checks, 4 FAILING and 1 BROKEN (ammo: rankreallyhigh/rankers-still-failing-product). Their rank survives the product; it is not produced by it. Across the whole roster, 0 of 99 clients both rank somewhere and pass a majority of the checks (0 of 98) (ammo: rankreallyhigh/rank-and-pass-zero), and 98 of 99 sites fail a majority of the checks outright (78 of 99 measured) (ammo: rankreallyhigh/product-failing-verdict).

The double-fail segment, invisible everywhere AND failing the product, is 93 of 99 clients (73 of the 98 scanned) (ammo: rankreallyhigh/crossover-invisible-and-failing). That figure is reply-stage only: it never appears in cold copy beside the storefront headline, because two denominators in one email read as a typo. The owner's model line for cold copy carries one number: "Your business isn't the problem, it's RRH, they are failing almost every single client they manage, and we've got the data to prove it: all 280 storefronts they have under service fully audited and almost the entire book is failing online."

DemandBoost: sold marketing, delivered a photocopier

The book's lede, verbatim: "They paid for marketing. They got a photocopier." (ammo: demandboost/photocopier-headline). The receipts: 83 of 97 client sites run one of just 12 shared templates, and the largest template family contains the agency's own homepage (ammo: demandboost/templates-83-of-97, agency-on-same-template). 50 client sites load a stylesheet literally named css/demandboost.css (ammo: demandboost/css-named-after-agency). 172 distinct sentences of 15+ words appear word-for-word on 2 or more different client domains (ammo: demandboost/sentences-172). 37 "independent" sites share one accessibility-widget vendor account (ammo: demandboost/userway-shared-account). 90 of 97 homepages link back to demandboost.com from the client's own footer (ammo: demandboost/footer-links-90).

The map outcome of photocopied marketing: 82 of 95 scanned clients do not own "chiropractor near me", the median client holds a 5.79% share and sits at average position #8.86, and 48 of 95 never hit #1 at a single pin (ammo: demandboost/not-own-phrase, median-share, median-position, never-rank-one). The Joint Chiropractic, a national franchise not on the roster, is the reigning leader on 23 of the 95 client grids (ammo: demandboost/joint-leads-23).

The speed outcome: all 97 of 97 sites fail Google's 2.5 second phone bar, median 10.5 seconds, worst 52.7 seconds, while the desktop median is 2.4 seconds (ammo: demandboost/speed-all-97-fail, speed-median, speed-worst, speed-desktop-gap). The sites were built for the screen patients do not search on, which is why an owner who "checked the site and it looked fine" checked it on a desktop.

And the tell that unravels the trophy case: the 13 winners have a median of 200 Google reviews against a roster median of 49; the wins correlate with an asset the patients built, not with the agency's work, and as the book itself says, that asset moves with the practice, not with the invoice (ammo: demandboost/winners-carried-by-reviews, reviews-move-with-you). Where the agency did rank things, it ranked the wrong ones: 24 clients hold 0% of the money phrase but a share of a narrow niche phrase; El Cajon Auto Accident Injury Clinic holds 0% of "chiropractor near me" and 100% of "auto accident chiropractor" (ammo: demandboost/wrong-phrase-24). Perfect score on the empty street, zero on the street with the traffic.

EyeCarePro: industrialized the neglect

The largest book, and the mechanism is scale itself. 577 of 708 measured sites sit on one shared template family that spans 1,220 sites book-wide (ammo: eyecarepro/shared-template, template-family-size). 655 client sites ship the agency's own logo asset (ammo: eyecarepro/agency-logo-on-client-sites). The map result is a coin flip: 358 of 710 practices outside the top 3 on their own neighborhood map (ammo: eyecarepro/outside-top3), only 165 of 710 lead their own grid (ammo: eyecarepro/grid-leaders), and 91 practices pay a local-search agency while their Google Business Profile sits unclaimed, a free fix that takes an afternoon (ammo: eyecarepro/gbp-unclaimed).

The opponent is corporate: 288 of the 710 client maps are led by a national chain, 654 have a chain inside the top 10, and America's Best alone leads 82 client grids (ammo: eyecarepro/chain-led-maps, chain-top10, americas-best). The agency's clients are losing their own neighborhoods to discount chains.

The product floor: zero of 710 sites earned SOUND, 603 carry FAILING or BROKEN, 94 fail all six checks, 702 of 706 miss Google's phone bar with a median paint of 9,387 ms, 625 of 708 have no llms.txt, and 267 of 709 lack LocalBusiness schema entirely (ammo: eyecarepro/zero-sound-sites, failing-or-broken, fail-count-distribution, lcp-over-google-bar, median-mobile-lcp, no-llms-txt, no-localbusiness-schema). The two-tier detail that proves it is neglect rather than ignorance: every llms.txt found (83 sites) sits on the agency's newer platform; the 576 sites on the older build have zero (ammo: eyecarepro/llms-only-on-v4). The agency knows how; it has not done it for the paying majority. And 211 sites have no booking path at all, marketing spend landing patients on a page where they cannot book (ammo: eyecarepro/no-booking-path).

Bottom line, the book's own cross-tab: 358 of 710 fail on BOTH axes, and 710 of 710 have at least one measured deficiency (ammo: eyecarepro/both-fail). In this book, literally nobody is safe.

5. What this means for the owner of one dispensary

The meaning ledger. Reps speak from the right-hand column, never from the left. Left is the measurement and its receipt; right is what it means to the person who owns one store, in money, customers or pride, one line each, one number at most. Raw milliseconds and test scores never leave this table; their translations do. The lines are whole-book by design: the reader's own row is proof of the pattern, never the reader's deficiency.

Measurement (receipt)What it means to the owner, said the way a rep says it
275 of 280 storefronts never appear on "weed near me" (ammo: rankreallyhigh/headline-storefronts-invisible)Customers: people on your own block type the weed search every day, and the company you pay made sure not one of them finds you.
94 of 99 clients invisible at every location; 93 of 98 scanned plus 1 unscanned (ammo: rankreallyhigh/clients-invisible-everywhere)Pride: this is not one bad location or one bad month; it is nearly every client they have, at every door. You are the pattern, not the exception.
139 of 280 in the top 3 on "dispensary near me" against 275 of 280 gone on "weed near me" (ammo: rankreallyhigh/dispensary-top3, headline-storefronts-invisible)Money: they got you found on the phrase fewer customers use and erased on the one most of them use. The invoice is the same either way.
"weed" 66 against "dispensary" 35, every one of 53 weeks (ammo: rankreallyhigh/trends-weed-average, trends-dispensary-average, trends-weed-wins-every-week)Customers: every single week for the past year more people typed weed than dispensary. Not one exception. Your agency built for the smaller crowd.
"weed" peaked at 100 the week of 2026-04-19 (ammo: rankreallyhigh/trends-peak-week)Money: the biggest weed week of the year was in April. An invisible storefront costs the most in the week that matters most.
153 different grid leaders; a client leads its own grid once (ammo: rankreallyhigh/grid-leaders-distinct, grid-leader-client-self)Pride: somebody wins your neighborhood every day. In the whole book it is a paying client of this agency exactly once.
Weed Man Lawn Care leads 11 grids (ammo: rankreallyhigh/grid-leader-weed-man-lawn-care)Pride: the business ranking above dispensaries on the weed search mows lawns. That is what the retainer bought.
A look-alike-character spam listing leads 10 grids (ammo: rankreallyhigh/grid-leader-spoofed-listing)Money: a fake listing with fake letters is collecting customers ahead of real stores. Your agency should be reporting it, not losing to it.
"Quincy Cannabis Co Weed Dispensary" leads 9 grids (ammo: rankreallyhigh/grid-leader-quincy)Customers: the winners put the word customers type in the listing name. Nobody on your invoice did that for you.
96 of 99 sites on one theme, logo swapped (ammo: rankreallyhigh/template-family-01)Pride: your website is one of 96 with the logo swapped. I can send you the list of sites literally identical to yours, same theme, same broken stats, and RRH doesn't care.
95 of 99 on one menu plugin; 92 on one version (ammo: rankreallyhigh/plugin-joint-ecommerce, plugin-version-cohort)Money: you are not a client, you are a unit in a fleet. What breaks on one of their sites breaks on all of them, and nobody fixes it for one.
98 of 99 sites fail Google's 2.5 second phone bar (76 of 77 measured); typical 9.2 seconds (ammo: rankreallyhigh/speed-lcp-over-google-bar, speed-median-load)Money: a customer on a phone stares at your blank screen for about nine seconds. Most leave at three. That sale walked to the store that loaded.
35 of 77 measured sites over 10 seconds (ammo: rankreallyhigh/speed-over-10-seconds)Customers: 35 of the sites they built keep a phone waiting more than ten seconds. Time yours on your own phone, right now.
93 of 99 fail Google's phone speed test; 17 never painted a screen (ammo: rankreallyhigh/psi-mobile-fail, psi-never-painted)Pride: Google's own free test fails almost every site they built, and seventeen never showed Google a single screen. Run it on your domain; it takes a minute.
54 of 99 sites with no reachable product page (52 of 96 read) (ammo: rankreallyhigh/zero-products-fault)Money: Google cannot find a single product on your menu. Someone searching for exactly what you sell cannot be sent to you.
Median 12 product pages where any are reachable (ammo: rankreallyhigh/product-pages-median)Customers: even where Google can read the menu it sees a dozen products, not a store.
97 of 99 sites with pages missing a description; 97 of 99 with pages missing a heading (ammo: rankreallyhigh/onpage-missing-description-sites, onpage-no-h1-sites)Customers: pages with no headline and no sales line. Google guesses what you sell, and it guesses wrong.
95 of 99 sites repeat titles; 275 pages share one title on a single site (ammo: rankreallyhigh/onpage-duplicate-title-sites, onpage-worst-title-repeat)Pride: one title pasted across hundreds of pages is what "templated to death" looks like. Pages fight each other for one spot and all of them lose.
752 of 14,060 menu pages clear the basics; typical menu page 26 words (ammo: rankreallyhigh/menu-pass-basics, menu-word-median)Money: your menu pages carry fewer words than a receipt. About one menu page in twenty is fit to rank, and the menu is the product.
97 of 99 carry LocalBusiness schema; 1 of 99 tags a product (ammo: rankreallyhigh/schema-localbusiness-present, schema-homepage-product-tagging)Customers: they told Google you are a business. They never told Google what you sell. Concede the first half; the second half is the point.
65 of 99 fail contrast; 34 of 99 fail tap targets; 41 of 99 block pinch-zoom (ammo: rankreallyhigh/a11y-color-contrast, a11y-tap-targets, a11y-pinch-zoom-disabled)Money: buttons too small to tap and text too faint to read on a phone are lost orders, and faint text and unnamed links are the two things a lawsuit letter names first.
39 of 39 multi-location clients invisible everywhere (ammo: rankreallyhigh/multi-location-all-invisible)Pride: every client with more than one store is invisible at all of them. More storefronts did not buy one more spot.
Two largest chains, 36 storefronts, 0 found (ammo: rankreallyhigh/chains-two-largest-zero)Money: thirty-six storefronts of buying power and not one in the top three anywhere. Size buys these clients nothing.
Puff Cannabis, 4.9 stars, 7,746 reviews, 10 stores, 0 spots (ammo: rankreallyhigh/pattern-reviews-do-not-rescue)Pride: thousands of customers built a near-perfect reputation, and the agency built a listing nobody can find. Reviews do not rescue a phrase gap.
5 of 5 rankers still fail the product (ammo: rankreallyhigh/rankers-still-failing-product)Customers: even the five that rank got a failing website. Their rank survives the product; it was not produced by it.
0 of 99 rank and pass (0 of 98) (ammo: rankreallyhigh/rank-and-pass-zero)Pride: nobody on this roster gets both the map and a working website. Nobody.
98 of 99 sites fail a majority of the product checks (78 of 99 measured) (ammo: rankreallyhigh/product-failing-verdict)Money: the product you pay for every month fails its own checks. You are paying for something that is holding your business back.
93 of 99 invisible AND failing (73 of 98) (ammo: rankreallyhigh/crossover-invisible-and-failing)Reply stage only, never cold: nearly the whole book is invisible on the search customers type and paying monthly for a failing website. You are not the exception.
1 client never scanned: no storefront listing resolved (ammo: rankreallyhigh/client-never-scanned)Customers, said only to that row: we could not even find a storefront to measure. Not ranked low. Not there.
560 scan reports, 98 competitor reports, one scan tag (ammo: rankreallyhigh/receipts-scan-reports, receipts-competitor-reports, receipts-scan-tag)Pride: every number is one click away on a site we do not own. You do not have to trust us; you only have to look.

Two disciplines on this table. First, one number per cold email, so a rep picks the one row that fits the reader and leaves the rest for the next touch. Second, the first two emails stay whole-book; the reader's own row enters at email 3 as proof of the pattern, never as the reader's deficiency.

6. Why leaving RankReallyHigh is the rational move and what the fix looks like

Register: evidence, not opinion. Nothing in this section calls anyone a bad person. It puts what the client pays for beside what the record shows they get, on the same instrument, and lets the gap stand.

What they pay for. Two things: a presence on the local map when a customer nearby searches, and a website that turns that customer into a sale.

What the record shows they get. On the map: 275 of 280 storefronts absent on the phrase most customers type, and 94 of 99 clients absent at every door (93 of 98 scanned plus 1 unscanned) (ammo: rankreallyhigh/headline-storefronts-invisible, clients-invisible-everywhere). On the website: 98 of 99 past Google's phone speed bar (76 of 77 measured), 54 of 99 with no product page a search engine can reach (52 of 96 read), 98 of 99 failing a majority of the product checks (78 of 99 measured) (ammo: rankreallyhigh/speed-lcp-over-google-bar, zero-products-fault, product-failing-verdict). Both together: 0 of 99 clients get the map and a working site (0 of 98) (ammo: rankreallyhigh/rank-and-pass-zero).

Why staying is the irrational option, in evidence terms.

  1. The failure is structural, not per-client. One theme on 96 of 99 sites, one plugin on 95, one version on 92 (ammo: rankreallyhigh/template-family-01, plugin-joint-ecommerce, plugin-version-cohort). A fix to one client's site is a fleet decision the agency has not made for anyone: 0 of 99 escaped. A client waiting for their turn is waiting for a turn the record says does not come.
  2. The phrase choice is book-wide. The agency's own strength, 139 of 280 storefronts in the top 3 on "dispensary near me", shows it can rank a storefront (ammo: rankreallyhigh/dispensary-top3). It ranked them on the word customers type less, every week of the year (ammo: rankreallyhigh/trends-weed-wins-every-week). That is a targeting decision, and it was made for the whole book.
  3. The winners' rank did not come from the site. The five that rank all run failing or broken websites (ammo: rankreallyhigh/rankers-still-failing-product); the listing that leads 9 grids carries the customer word in its name (ammo: rankreallyhigh/grid-leader-quincy); Puff Cannabis has 7,746 reviews and no spots (ammo: rankreallyhigh/pattern-reviews-do-not-rescue). What ranks a dispensary is the listing and the phrase; what the agency sells is the site, and the site fails.
  4. Size and reputation do not buy a way out. 39 of 39 multi-location clients invisible everywhere; 36 storefronts across the two largest chains, 0 found (ammo: rankreallyhigh/multi-location-all-invisible, chains-two-largest-zero).

Said to the prospect: this is not opinion and it is not a personal attack on anyone; it just is what it is. Same stores, same place IDs, same instrument, both phrases, every storefront, and the reports sit on a site we do not own. Once you see the data you cannot unsee it.

What a working local presence is. The bar, in plain words, with the proof that each part is reachable on this exact stack:

  • Found on the customer phrase at every storefront. Inside the three-name pack on "weed near me" across the storefront's own neighborhood, not on one lucky corner. The market is winnable: 153 different businesses win a grid somewhere, and Roots LA and Roslindale Cannabis are found at all 121 of their 121 spots (ammo: rankreallyhigh/grid-leaders-distinct, the-only-five-found). The listing carries the word customers type, the way the 9-grid leader's does (ammo: rankreallyhigh/grid-leader-quincy), and the storefront's business identity is complete and consistent everywhere it is published.
  • A site whose main content loads in under 2.5 seconds on a phone. Google's bar, not ours. 6 of 99 sites in this book already pass Google's phone speed test, so the bar is reachable on this stack (ammo: rankreallyhigh/psi-pass); the typical site is 3.7 times over it (ammo: rankreallyhigh/speed-bar-multiple).
  • Real product pages a search engine can reach. Every product on its own page, reachable by a plain link, with its own title, its own description, a heading and real words: the same five basics the book's menu check uses (ammo: rankreallyhigh/menu-pass-basics). Not cards drawn in by a script inside boxes that are not links (ammo: rankreallyhigh/zero-products-mechanism), and not a dozen pages standing in for a store (ammo: rankreallyhigh/product-pages-median). Business identity in the code, which they already mostly have (ammo: rankreallyhigh/schema-localbusiness-present), plus the product tagging that 1 of 99 has (ammo: rankreallyhigh/schema-homepage-product-tagging).

The fix is the work. Nothing above is a trick or a setting. It is the listing and its name built around the phrase customers type; the site moved off the fleet template and made to load; the menu rebuilt so every product is a real page Google can reach; the headings, titles and descriptions written once per page instead of pasted. The free audit does not do the work; it shows the gap, storefront by storefront and page by page, against the bar above, with the prospect's own third-party reports open on the screen.

The offer, and only the offer. Two formats, the prospect's choice: a 15-minute quick audit, or a 1-hour full audit that includes education and a presentation of our services only if the fit is real, decided in the first part of the call (source: the offer section of sales/_campaign-brief-audit-outreach.md, mirrored in the ammo sheet's funnel block). The owner's shape for the close, verbatim: "We can do this two ways. If you want the quick version, I can show you in 15 minutes every way RankReallyHigh is failing you. If you've got more time, I can tell you more about us, why we're auditing other agencies' entire books of business, and how we can help fix the problems RRH is charging you for." Taking the call never means the prospect gets pitched; we do not work with everyone, only when the fit is real for both sides. The prospect gets the benefit of the call either way, and the file stays free and identical either way.

What a rep never does here. No adjectives the file does not earn; no promise of a rank or a date; no name-calling; no invitation to opt out in sales prose (the legal minimum lives only in the muted signature line); no announcing the end of the outreach. The ask stays live until they book or say stop, and stop is honored instantly and forever.

7. Why the record holds up under attack

Reps should understand the design choices that make the books hard to rebut, because prospects and agencies will probe them:

  • Whole rosters, not samples. 710 of 710, 95 of 97, and on RRH every one of the agency's 99 active clients and every one of their 280 storefronts, not a chosen subset (ammo: eyecarepro/total-practices, demandboost/clients-total, rankreallyhigh/book-scope-clients, book-scope-storefronts).
  • One phrase, declared. No phrase-shopping; the excluded scans are disclosed (ammo: eyecarepro/one-phrase). On RRH the second phrase is declared too, and run over the same storefronts on purpose, because the comparison is the argument (ammo: rankreallyhigh/flip-same-storefronts-both-phrases).
  • Self-audit in public. The ECP book flags 28 of its own grids as too tight to contain a real contest, discloses 5 purged scans and 2 uncaptured sites (ammo: eyecarepro/self-audit-disclosed). The RRH book prints its own ruling and the measured twin beside every ruled figure, names the 1 client it could never scan on that client's own page, and records that it was filed 2026-08-09 and amended 2026-08-26 when it was rebuilt on the active client set (ammo: rankreallyhigh/client-never-scanned-honest-line, book-filed). The desk publishes its own weaknesses; the agencies publish none.
  • Winners are named winners. All 13 DB winners and all 5 RRH rankers are called what they are, with their remaining findings still printed (ammo: demandboost/winner-cards-still-flagged, rankreallyhigh/the-only-five-found, rankers-still-failing-product). The concessions are said out loud: 97 of 99 carry LocalBusiness schema, 6 of 99 pass the speed test, 46 of 99 pass the accessibility bar, 78 of 99 have one good menu page (ammo: rankreallyhigh/schema-localbusiness-present, psi-pass, a11y-pass-bar, menu-sites-with-passing-page).
  • Third-party receipts. Every scanned business resolves to a localrankingtracker.com report the prospect can open without trusting us; on RRH, 560 scan reports and 98 competitor reports under one scan tag (ammo: rankreallyhigh/receipts-scan-reports, receipts-competitor-reports, receipts-scan-tag).
  • No pay-to-play, by construction. Verbatim from the hub: "Nobody pays to be excluded and nobody pays to be included." and "Independent of every agency it measures: this desk is not affiliated with, endorsed by, or compensated by any agency named in these files, and nobody named in a file can pay to change one." The RRH book's own footer: "An independent performance analysis of the Rank Really High client roster, not published by or affiliated with Rank Really High." (ammo: rankreallyhigh/integrity-anchor-verbatim, integrity-independence-verbatim, book-boilerplate-verbatim). Files are free; nobody named can pay to change one.
8. Glossary

Plain words for every term the arsenal still uses. Where the data word and the prospect word differ, both are given; prospect copy uses the plain one.

The map and the scan

  • Map pack: the three named businesses shown with the map at the top of a local search on a phone; positions 1 to 3. The battleground.
  • Spot (pin, in the data): one place on the map where the search was run as if a customer stood there. Prospect copy says "spots" or "places"; the data says "pins". The words pins, grid, ranking points, data points and search rankings never appear in a prospect sentence.
  • Grid: the full set of 121 spots, 11 by 11, laid over one storefront's neighborhood. Prospect copy says "your own neighborhood" or "every spot around you".
  • Grid radius: how far out from the storefront the grid reaches, 1 to 15 miles, sized to each market and printed in the book.
  • Map point: one spot on one grid; 121 per grid, 33,880 in the RRH book (ammo: rankreallyhigh/book-scope-map-points).
  • Found spots (found pins): the spots where the business appears at any rank. Zero means invisible on its own map for that phrase.
  • Rank-1 spots / turf: spots where the business is the first name in the pack.
  • Top-3 share (share of local voice, SOLV): the percentage of spots where the business is inside the pack. DB's WINNER bar is 50% or more.
  • Average rank position: the business's average place in the map results across the spots where it was found.
  • Grid leader: the business that holds the top spot most broadly on a grid; someone always leads. Coverage is the share of the grid that leader holds.
  • Competitors on the grid: every other business that showed up anywhere across the 121 spots; RRH's typical grid has 39 (ammo: rankreallyhigh/dispensary-median-competitors).
  • Flagship storefront / flagship grid: for a client with several storefronts, the one the book uses as the representative row (its rating, review count, grid leader and report links); every storefront still has its own grid.
  • Place ID: Google's own permanent identifier for one listing; scanning the same place IDs on two phrases is what makes the flip a fair comparison.
  • Scan report / competitor report: the third-party pages on localrankingtracker.com that every scanned business links to; the scan report shows the grid, the competitor report shows who holds each spot. Both are delivered on reply or at the audit, never linked cold.
  • Scan tag: the one identifier every RRH report sits under; one tag means one scan run, nothing stitched from separate sessions (ammo: rankreallyhigh/receipts-scan-tag).
  • Google Trends index: Google's public 0 to 100 score of how often a word is searched, against the busiest week in the window.

The units and the verdicts

  • Client: one dispensary business; 99 in the book. Site: that client's one website; 99. Storefront: one physical location with its own grid; 280. Never swapped.
  • Chain / multi-location client: a client with two or more storefronts; 39 in the book, all invisible at every storefront (ammo: rankreallyhigh/multi-location-all-invisible).
  • Map verdict (RRH): NOWHERE, scanned at every storefront and found at none; PACK_WITH_TURF, in the pack and first at some spots; NOT_MEASURED, no storefront listing could be resolved, so no grid was ever run. ECP adds POCKET_NO_REACH and PACK_NEVER_FIRST; DB uses WINNER, PARTIAL, INVISIBLE.
  • Segment: the outreach sheet's grouping of the same thing: NOWHERE, FOUND, NOT_MEASURED.
  • Dispensary standing: the client's result on the second phrase, "dispensary near me": LEADS, IN_PACK, BEATEN, UNRANKED.
  • Product verdict: SOUND, WEAK, FAILING, BROKEN, from six yes-or-no website checks (shared template, recycled copy, llms.txt, phone speed score, phone load time, LocalBusiness schema). Failing a majority is FAILING; failing nearly all is BROKEN.
  • Measured versus published (the ruling and the twin): measured is what the instrument returned; published is the same figure under the 99-base ruling, where a thing that could not be measured is scored as failing what it could not be measured for. A published figure is always said with its measured twin the first time.
  • Finding line: one typed, countable problem statement on a hub listing card. Combined line: the card's one-sentence closer, generated from the row's own result.
  • Verdict: the typed judgment per business, one per axis.
  • The crossover / the double fail: invisible on the map AND failing the product; reply-stage only in outreach.

The RRH case words

  • The smaller door: the agency optimized "dispensary near me", the phrase worth about half of "weed near me" by Google's own count (ammo: rankreallyhigh/argument-smaller-term-verbatim, trends-note-verbatim).
  • The flip: the same 280 storefronts ranking on one phrase and vanishing on the other; the proof that the gap is the phrase, not a broken scan.
  • The weed search / the search your customers actually type: prospect-copy names for "weed near me". Never list an unmeasured phrase as measured.
  • The pattern: the whole-book shape (nearly every client invisible, nearly every site failing) that the reader's own row is proof of.
  • Spoofed listing: a spam map listing whose name is typed in look-alike foreign characters to pass as a real business; leads 10 RRH grids (ammo: rankreallyhigh/grid-leader-spoofed-listing).
  • Fleet: one theme, one plugin, one plugin version across the book; the reason a fix for one client is a decision for all of them.

The website words

  • Template family / shared template: a group of sites sharing one design skeleton; membership is a measured fingerprint, not an opinion. RRH's is family-01, the WordPress theme "weedpress".
  • Menu plugin (joint-ecommerce): the add-on that draws the product menu on the site; the same one on 95 of 99 RRH sites.
  • Phone load time (LCP, Largest Contentful Paint, in the data): how long the main content of a page takes to appear on a phone; Google's bar is 2.5 seconds. Prospect copy says seconds, never milliseconds.
  • Mobile PageSpeed (PSI, PageSpeed Insights): Google's free phone speed test, scored 0 to 100; under 50 is the book's failing bar. Prospect copy says "Google's own free speed test", never the score.
  • Never painted: the site showed Google's test tool no screen at all, so it could not be scored; 17 RRH sites (ammo: rankreallyhigh/psi-never-painted). Scored as failing under the ruling.
  • Crawl / crawler: a program (Screaming Frog, a standard tool) that follows every link on a site and records every page it reaches. Crawl blocked (403): the site refused the crawler; scored as failing every crawl-derived check.
  • Reachable product page: a page for one product that a search engine can reach by following an ordinary link. Zero products: the crawl reached none; on RRH the cause, where a menu was read, is product cards drawn in by a script inside boxes that are not links.
  • Menu page: a page listing products; 14,060 on RRH. Five basics: indexable, its own title, its own description, a heading, and 200 words; 752 menu pages clear all five (ammo: rankreallyhigh/menu-pass-basics).
  • Thin / low-content page: a page with too few words to say what it is about; on RRH the thin pages are the menu pages, typical length 26 words.
  • Non-indexable page: a page carrying a tag that tells Google not to list it; it cannot rank no matter what is on it.
  • Page title: the words Google shows as the blue link. Duplicate title: the same title on two or more pages of one site, pages fighting for one spot.
  • Meta description: the sales line Google shows under the blue link; blank means Google writes it for you.
  • Main heading (H1): the headline on the page that says what the page is about.
  • LocalBusiness schema: code that tells Google a site is a local business; the check RRH mostly passes. Product tagging: the same kind of code for what the store sells; 1 of 99 RRH homepages has it.
  • llms.txt: a proposed file describing a site to AI answer engines; no major engine has committed to reading it. The check measures presence or absence only, and reps must not claim absence makes a site invisible to AI answers.
  • Accessibility audit: Google's checks that a site works for everyone on a phone: color contrast (text faint against its background), link name (a link a screen reader cannot describe), pinch-to-zoom (blocked zoom), tap targets (buttons too small or too close), unlabeled images (pictures with no text label). 90 or better passes the book's bar.

The book and the desk

  • Book / dossier: one agency's whole client roster, measured and published; three filed on the hub (ammo: rankreallyhigh/hub-books).
  • Hub: the publisher's site that carries the books and one listing page per measured business; 906 businesses, 131,285 map points (ammo: rankreallyhigh/hub-businesses, hub-map-points).
  • Listing page: the public page for one business on the hub, with its verdicts and finding lines.
  • Docket: the book's case code; RRH-146 is a case number, not a count of anything.
  • Filed / amended: the date the book was published and the date it was rebuilt on the active client set.
  • Receipts: the third-party reports, the book's own verbatim lines, and the ammo sheet's stat ids; anything a prospect can check without trusting us.
  • Ammo sheet: the generated file of verified stats every number in the arsenal traces to; a number not in it is not said.
  • Lead sheet: the outreach file with one row per client, its measured and published columns, and its contact columns; contact columns never appear in sales conversations or client-facing material.

The outreach words

  • Free audit / second opinion / audit call: what the desk offers, in two formats: the 15-minute quick audit and the 1-hour full audit. The prospect gets the benefit; taking the call never means a pitch.
  • The door: the same close on every touch: free audit, fifteen minutes or an hour, "yes?".
  • Armed row: a client row where a named person sits on a verified personal mailbox; the greeting uses the first name. Unarmed row: no name; the greeting uses the business name. The only fork inside the ladder.
  • Navigator row: a verified general business inbox; the sequence's only goal is routing to the decision maker.
  • Whole-book touch: an email that speaks only of the agency's whole book, no per-row claim; emails 1 and 2 are whole-book only.
  • The knife / the one savage true line: the single line per email that lands the wound, aimed at the agency, weaponized to the row.
  • Meaning law / one number: state what a measurement means (money, customers, pride), then offer the data as proof; one number at most per email; raw milliseconds, scores and index values never in a prospect sentence.
  • Reply flip / aggressive sequence: any reply moves the row to the daily sequence until it books or says stop.
  • STOP / suppression: any stop request is honored instantly and permanently. CASL row: the one Canadian client (theniagaraherbalist.com), suppressed at send time under Canada's anti-spam law.
  • Links ladder: resources are described in text in cold email and delivered on reply: rung 1 the prospect's own listing page, rung 2 the row screenshot, rung 3 the third-party reports, rung 4 booking.
275 of 280 storefronts
Type "weed near me" from anywhere on a storefront's own map grid and 275 of the 280 storefronts scanned never appear at all.
5 of 280 storefronts
Only 5 of the 280 storefronts show up anywhere on their own grid for "weed near me".
94 of 99 (measured 93/98)
Counting clients rather than storefronts, 94 of the 99 client dispensaries are invisible on "weed near me" at every location they operate.
98.2%
Put as a share, 98.2% of the storefronts in the book are invisible on "weed near me".

THE PHRASE weed near me · open the live book

Fence-sitter questions

Fifteen questions for RankReallyHigh

An arming list for a dispensary owner who is deciding whether to stay. Ask these of your agency, on a scheduled call, with your own file open in front of you.

This list is handed over at the free audit, or sent on request after you have replied to the desk. It is never sent cold.

Where your file is: rankreallyhigh/, and your own page is at l/rankreallyhigh/<your domain>.html. Every number below comes from that published book and its data sheet (sales/_ammo/rankreallyhigh.json, stat ids in brackets), and every storefront claim on your page links to a third-party report on localrankingtracker.com that you can open without us. The book covers all 99 active RankReallyHigh clients, 99 websites, 280 storefronts, filed 2026-08-09 and amended 2026-08-26. [book-scope-clients] [book-scope-storefronts] [book-filed]

Who we are, stated plainly: the file is "not published by or affiliated with Rank Really High." We compete with them and we publish the receipts. The files are free. Nobody named in a file can pay to change one. Nobody pays to be excluded and nobody pays to be included. [book-boilerplate-verbatim] [integrity-independence-verbatim] [integrity-anchor-verbatim]

One more thing, so the register is clear: none of this is opinion and none of it is a personal attack on anyone. It is a measurement, and it just is what it is. Once you see the data you cannot unsee it.

How to use the list: pick three questions, not fifteen. Ask, then stop talking. Each entry tells you the receipt behind the question, the dodge you should expect, and what a real answer would look like. If you hear the dodge, you have your answer too.

1. "Show me, on a map, where my store ranks for the weed search at each of my locations, today, while I'm on the phone."

The receipt: we already did this for the whole roster: 280 storefronts, 121 spots around each one, and 275 of 280 never appeared anywhere on their own map. Counting businesses, 94 of the 99 clients are invisible at every location they run; 93 of the 98 that could be scanned, plus the one that could not. Your page links your own third-party report; 98 of them are published, one per scanned client. [book-scope-pins-per-grid] [headline-storefronts-invisible] [clients-invisible-everywhere] [receipts-competitor-reports]

The predicted dodge: a pivot to an organic-keyword dashboard, a "visibility score", or "rankings fluctuate daily, a snapshot means nothing."

A real answer looks like: a live map with your store on it, showing your rank at your front door and a mile out, on the search your customers actually type, generated while you watch. A spreadsheet is not a map. A dashboard pivot is the dodge.

2. "Of all your cannabis clients, how many rank top 3 on the weed search anywhere, and can I talk to three of them?"

The receipt: the published answer is 5 of 280 storefronts, 5 of 99 clients, and the file names all five: Roots LA, Roslindale Cannabis, Nile Cannabis, Quincy Cannabis Co, Clouditude. [headline-storefronts-found] [clients-ranking-somewhere] [the-only-five-found]

The predicted dodge: one hand-picked success story, usually one of those five, offered as if it were the norm.

A real answer looks like: a rate across the whole client base, then references. One cherry-picked name out of 280 storefronts does not contradict the file. It confirms the base rate.

3. "Which phrase did you optimize my listing for, and what is its demand next to the weed search?"

The receipt: the book's core argument, in its own six words: "The agency optimized the smaller term." Google's own data: customers type "weed" about twice as often as "dispensary", every single week for the past year, not one exception, and "cannabis" trails both. The biggest week of the year was 2026-04-19. [argument-smaller-term-verbatim] [trends-weed-average] [trends-dispensary-average] [trends-cannabis-average] [trends-weed-wins-every-week] [trends-peak-week]

The predicted dodge: "we target a broad portfolio of keywords" with no numbers attached, "our data shows dispensary converts better" with no data shown, or a critique of the desk's chart: "Trends indexes the word weed, not the phrase, and an index is not volume." The critique is true as far as it goes, and the file cites Trends as exactly what it is, Google's published term-level interest with the source printed. A critique of somebody else's chart with no numbers of their own attached is still a dodge.

A real answer looks like: their phrase, named, with its demand next to the weed search, from a source you can open yourself, in writing. If they produce real phrase-level volumes, take it, and then ask the follow-up their volumes cannot dodge: on the phrase they did choose, 141 of 280 storefronts in this book sit at rank 4 or worse. Whichever phrase is bigger, ask which half of their own book you are in. [dispensary-beaten]

4. "Who is the number one result on my own map right now, and why is it them and not me?"

The receipt: across the 280 maps, 153 different businesses lead somewhere, and a client of this agency leads its own map exactly once. The leaders include Weed Man Lawn Care, a lawn-care franchise, on 11 maps, and a spam listing typed in look-alike foreign characters on 10 more. Your page names your own leader and how much of your map it holds. [grid-leaders-distinct] [grid-leader-client-self] [grid-leader-weed-man-lawn-care] [grid-leader-spoofed-listing]

The predicted dodge: "maps are full of spam, nothing anyone can do", said as if that closes the subject.

A real answer looks like: your leader named without you prompting, a specific reason they win, and a specific plan against it. If they call your leader spam and say complaints are already filed, do not accept a date on faith: ask them to open the complaint and its acknowledgment on screen, the same way the file opens its scans. And keep the scale in view: the two junk leaders the file names hold 11 and 10 of the 280 maps. Read the name on your own page and judge for yourself whether you are losing to spam or to a competitor. "Nothing can be done" is contradicted by the 153 businesses currently doing it.

5. "Is my website a custom build? What theme is it on, and how many of your other clients run the same one?"

The receipt: 96 of the 99 client sites are one build, the WordPress theme "weedpress", with the logo swapped. The stack underneath matches: 95 of 99 sites run the same menu plugin and 92 of them sit on the exact same version, 1.38.1. [template-family-01] [template-theme-weedpress] [plugin-joint-ecommerce] [plugin-version-cohort]

The predicted dodge: "it's our proprietary platform" or "the design is customized for your brand."

A real answer looks like: the theme name, said out loud. You can check it yourself in your own page source before the call. A logo swap on a theme shared across 96 of the 99 client sites is not a custom build, and a real answer would not pretend it is.

6. "On 'dispensary near me', the phrase you did optimize, which half of your book am I in?"

The receipt: on the agency's own chosen phrase, 139 of 280 storefronts crack the top 3 and 141 sit at rank 4 or worse. The tail is long: 38 storefronts rank 11th to 20th, 36 rank 21st or worse, and 7 do not rank on a single spot, on the phrase they pay to win. For every storefront the book links both scan reports, one per phrase, 560 in all. [dispensary-top3] [dispensary-beaten] [dispensary-rank-distribution] [dispensary-unranked] [receipts-scan-reports]

The predicted dodge: an average position blended across keywords, or a hand-picked ranking report from the good half of the book.

A real answer looks like: your rank on your own map, on their phrase, shown on a map, today. The book splits almost exactly in half on this, so the honest answer is one word and your page already shows it. An agency that loses the coin flip on the phrase it chose has no answer left about the phrase it ignored.

7. "How many of my pages can Google even read? How many have no description, no headline, or tell Google to skip them?"

The receipt: the crawl read 24,229 pages across the 96 client sites it could get into. 97 of 99 sites serve pages that tell Google not to index them, 95 of the 96 crawled; 4,736 pages in all, about a fifth of everything read. 97 of 99 sites have pages with no description under the blue link, 95 of 96 crawled; 4,335 pages. 97 of 99 have pages with no main headline, 95 of 96 crawled; 2,518 pages. 95 of 99 repeat one page title across their own pages, 93 of 96 crawled; the worst case is one title on 275 pages of a single site. 13,964 pages, more than half the crawl, are flagged as low content. [onpage-pages-crawled] [onpage-nonindexable-sites] [onpage-nonindexable-pages] [onpage-missing-description-sites] [onpage-missing-description-pages] [onpage-no-h1-sites] [onpage-no-h1-pages] [onpage-duplicate-title-sites] [onpage-worst-title-repeat] [onpage-low-content-pages]

The predicted dodge: "those are technical details, Google handles it" or a site-health score from their own dashboard with no page list behind it.

A real answer looks like: a page count, read from a crawl of your own site, with the pages named: how many Google is told to skip, how many have no description, how many have no headline. You pay for every one of those pages. A page Google cannot read costs the same to build as a page it can.

8. "What exactly do I pay for each month, itemized, and which line item owns my result on the weed search, the one my customers actually type?"

The receipt: the roster-wide outcome of those invoices, on the customer search: 275 of 280 storefronts never appear. On the website side, 98 of 99 client sites fail most of the checks they were measured on, 78 of 99 on the checks that returned a reading. [headline-storefronts-invisible] [product-failing-verdict]

The predicted dodge: bundle language, "it's a holistic SEO package, you can't separate the parts." Or, if your page shows a top-3 result on "dispensary near me", the map line item pointed at that: "it delivered, you are number one on the phrase we sold you."

A real answer looks like: line items, each mapped to a measurable outcome, with the map line held to the search in this question. The phrase matters because the book flips on it: the same 280 storefronts, the same Google listings, rank on "dispensary near me" and vanish on the weed search. A line item that delivered the smaller door did not buy the door with the traffic. If no line item owns the customer search, you have been paying for a result nobody was assigned to deliver. [flip-same-storefronts-both-phrases]

9. "Name one client, out of your 99, that got both: a top-3 result anywhere on the weed search and a website that does not grade failing in the file."

The receipt: 0 of 99 clients rank somewhere AND pass a majority of the product checks; 0 of the 98 that could be scanned. The 5 that rank are all failing on the website side: four fail 4 of 6 measured checks, one fails 5 of 6. [rank-and-pass-zero] [rankers-still-failing-product]

The predicted dodge: a hand-picked map winner offered without the website half, or an attack on the checks instead of a name.

A real answer looks like: a name. The product checks re-run from public surfaces: theme detection, plugin fingerprint, Google's phone test, schema. If the standard were rigged, one passing client would be easy to produce. There are zero in the book, so any name they offer can be checked against its own page in five minutes.

10. (For multi-location operators) "Show me one of my storefronts, any one, inside a map pack for the weed search."

The receipt: every one of the 39 clients in the book that runs two or more storefronts is invisible at every single one of them. The four largest chains, 15 to 18 storefronts each, were scanned at every storefront and none is found: FlynnStoned 18, Nirvana MI 18, Star Buds IL 17, Urban Wellness 15. The two largest were scanned at all 36 of their storefronts and found at none. [multi-location-all-invisible] [chains-largest-four-nowhere] [chains-two-largest-zero]

The predicted dodge: "multi-location is different, your own stores compete with each other" or "we prioritize your flagship."

A real answer looks like: one storefront, one pack, on the customer search, shown on a map. The question asks for a single example out of all your locations. If the answer to "any one" is none, the size of your account is buying you nothing here.

11. "My rating is 4.9 with thousands of reviews. Why am I still not on the map?"

The receipt: the file's exact counterexample is on this roster. Puff Cannabis: 4.9 Google stars, 7,746 reviews, 10 storefronts, and it appears at zero spots on the weed search at every single one of them. Its flagship map is led by Uniq Cannabis Weed Dispensary Monroe at 100 percent coverage. [pattern-reviews-do-not-rescue]

The predicted dodge: "keep collecting reviews, rankings follow reputation, it takes time."

A real answer looks like: an actual diagnosis of your listing on this search, because your reviews are demonstrably not the gap. A 4.9-star, 7,746-review business at zero spots is a strategy failure, not a reputation problem, and "give it time" has already had years.

12. "Same listing, same address: why do I show up for 'dispensary near me' and vanish on the weed search?"

The receipt: the same 280 storefronts, same Google listings, were scanned on both searches. On "dispensary near me", 139 of 280 crack the top 3. On the weed search, 5 of 280 show up at all. The flip is the phrase, not a data error, and Google's own data puts roughly twice the search interest on the phrase where the book vanishes. [flip-same-storefronts-both-phrases] [dispensary-top3] [headline-storefronts-found] [trends-note-verbatim]

The predicted dodge: "different phrases behave differently, that's just the algorithm."

A real answer looks like: an account of your listing specifically, ending in a dated plan you will see on a map. Expect a mechanism instead: "Google leans on listing names for that phrase, the weed results reward name-stuffing, and we will not play that game." Do not let it end there, because the file holds the counterexample: two of the five storefronts that do rank, Roots LA and Roslindale Cannabis, are found at all 121 of their 121 spots, and the names printed on their rows carry no "weed" at all. Both are clients of this same agency. A mechanism that explains who leads your map still has to explain why your listing is nowhere on it while two roster-mates rank clean, and what changes, by when. [the-only-five-found]

13. "Load my site on a phone right now, while I'm on the phone, timed. How many seconds until I can see the menu?"

The receipt: Google says a phone should show the main content within two and a half seconds. 98 of 99 client sites miss that bar, 76 of the 77 that returned a load time; the typical site takes 9.2 seconds, about 3.7 times the bar, and 35 sites take more than 10 seconds. On Google's own phone test, 93 of 99 sites either fail or never painted a screen at all, 76 of the 82 that got a score plus 17 that showed Google nothing. 6 of 99 sites pass, 6 of the 82 scored. [speed-lcp-over-google-bar] [speed-median-load] [speed-bar-multiple] [speed-over-10-seconds] [psi-mobile-fail] [psi-never-painted] [psi-pass]

The predicted dodge: "lab scores don't reflect real users", sometimes with a field-data screen offered in place of the stopwatch.
